If the queen is at c1, a move to b2 is a diagonal move.

Is the queen allowed to move there? Yes.

Let's re-examine the board with this mapping:
R8: . . . ♝ (Black Bishop) . . . .
R7: . . . . . . . .
R6: . . . . ♚ (Black King) . . ♟ (Black Pawn)

R4: . . . . . . . .

R2: . ♜ (Black Rook) . . . . . .
R1: . . ♛ (Black Queen) . . . . .

Wait! The Queen at c1 is ♛, which is a BLACK queen.
The King at b5 is ♔, which is a WHITE king.
So White only has the King at b5.
Black has a King at e6, a Queen at c1, a Rook at b2, a Bishop at d8, and pawns on d5, h5, h6.
This makes much more sense. The white king is in check by the black rook on b2.
The only legal moves for the white king are b5a6 and b5a4.

Between Ka6 and Ka4:
If Ka6, the king moves closer to the black bishop if it moves? No.
If Ka4, the king is on the edge.
Looking at the black pieces:
Queen at c1.
Rook at b2.
If the King moves to a4:
The rook at b2 can move to b4, checking again.
The queen at c1 can move to a3, checking.
If the King moves to a6:
The rook at b2 can move to b6, checking.
The queen at c1 can move to c6 or a3? No.

Generally, staying closer to the center or avoiding corners is better, but here the king is already on the edge of the board conceptually.
Actually, let's look at a6 vs a4.
If Ka6:
Black might play Rb6+? No, Rook is on b2. So Rb6 is a move. King is on a6. Rb6 is a check.
Then white king must move again.
If Ka4:
Black might play Rb4+? No, rook is on b2. So Rb4 is a move. King is on a4. Rb4 is a check.

Actually, in a lost position like this (White just has a king against a whole army), the goal is to prolong the game or hope for a stalemate.
But let's just pick one. a6 seems as good as a4.
Let's check if there are any other consequences.
Is there any way to get a stalemate?
If I move to a6, I'm further from the black pawns.
If I move to a4, I'm closer.
Usually, Ka6 is played.
